## Demo Video Framework - Implementation Plan

### 1. Package Structure

Convert `demo/` from a flat script to a proper Python package:

```
demo/
  __init__.py          # Package marker, version string
  __main__.py          # CLI entry point: python -m demo screenplay.yaml -o out.mp4
  schema.py            # Pydantic models for screenplay YAML + validation
  player.py            # Terminal renderer (refactored from current player.py)
  recorder.py          # Orchestrates asciinema recording of player.py
  camera.py            # Camera system: parses keyframes, generates ffmpeg filters
  pipeline.py          # End-to-end: record -> render frames -> apply camera -> encode
  scenes/
    __init__.py        # Scene registry + base class
    conversation.py    # user->thinking->tools->response scene (current play_scene)
    action.py          # pause, clear, print, banner, type_command (current play_action)
    # Future: transition.py, overlay.py, etc.
  scripts/             # Example screenplays (unchanged)
    example.yaml
    test_quick.yaml
```

**Total: 9 new/modified Python files** (plus keeping existing YAML scripts untouched).

### 2. Screenplay YAML Schema

The key addition is `camera` directives that can appear at the global level, per-scene, or as standalone events.

```yaml
# ── Metadata ─────────────────────────────
title: "Hermes Agent Demo"
skin: "default"                    # Use skin engine instead of hardcoded colors
output:
  width: 2560                      # Render resolution (2x for retina)
  height: 1440
  fps: 30
  font_size: 22
  font_family: "JetBrains Mono"
  theme: "dracula"                 # agg theme name
  final_width: 1280                # Downscale target for crisp text
  final_height: 720

# ── Global defaults ──────────────────────
typing_speed: 0.04
pause_between: 1.0

# ── Default camera (full frame) ─────────
camera:
  zoom: 1.0
  x: 0.5                          # Normalized center (0-1)
  y: 0.5
  ease: "ease-in-out"             # easing function

# ── Scenes ───────────────────────────────
scenes:
  - action: clear

  - action: banner
    model: "deephermes-3-llama-3.1-8b"
    camera:                        # Camera for this scene's duration
      zoom: 1.0                    # Show full banner

  - action: pause
    duration: 1.5

  - user: "What are the latest developments in AI agents?"
    thinking_time: 3.5
    camera:
      zoom: 1.8                    # Zoom into prompt area during typing
      y: 0.15                      # Focus on top portion
      at: "user_start"             # When to begin transition (default: scene start)
      duration: 0.8                # Transition duration in seconds
    tools:
      - icon: "🔍"
        verb: "search"
        detail: '"latest AI agents 2026"'
        duration: "2.1s"
        delay: 0.3
    response: |
      Here are the key developments...
    camera_response:               # Separate camera for response phase
      zoom: 1.4
      y: 0.6                      # Focus on response panel
      duration: 0.6

  # Standalone camera event (between scenes)
  - action: camera
    zoom: 1.0
    duration: 1.2
    ease: "ease-out"
```

**Camera coordinate system**: `x` and `y` are normalized 0.0-1.0 representing the center of the viewport. `zoom` is a multiplier (1.0 = full frame, 2.0 = 2x zoom showing half the frame in each dimension). This maps directly to ffmpeg's crop filter.

**Camera `at` markers**: For conversation scenes, the player emits timing markers:
- `scene_start` - when the scene begins
- `user_start` - when typing begins  
- `user_end` - when message is submitted
- `thinking_start` / `thinking_end`
- `tools_start` / `tools_end`  
- `response_start` / `response_end`
- `scene_end`

### 3. Timing System

The critical bridge between screenplay and ffmpeg is a **timing manifest** -- a JSON file the player writes as a side effect during recording.

**How it works:**

1. `player.py` gets a `--emit-timing` flag. When set, every `time.sleep()` and visual event writes a `(wall_clock_offset, marker_name)` tuple to a list.
2. After playback completes, the timing manifest is written as JSON:

```json
{
  "scenes": [
    {
      "index": 0,
      "type": "action",
      "action": "clear",
      "start_t": 0.0,
      "end_t": 0.16
    },
    {
      "index": 3,
      "type": "conversation", 
      "start_t": 2.99,
      "end_t": 18.42,
      "markers": {
        "user_start": 3.49,
        "user_end": 5.12,
        "thinking_start": 5.42,
        "thinking_end": 8.92,
        "tools_start": 8.92,
        "tools_end": 9.67,
        "response_start": 9.67,
        "response_end": 18.42
      }
    }
  ],
  "total_duration": 42.5
}
```

3. `camera.py` reads the screenplay camera directives + timing manifest and resolves every `at: "user_start"` reference to an absolute timestamp, then interpolates keyframes.

### 4. Recording Pipeline Architecture

`pipeline.py` orchestrates the full flow:

**Step 1: Record** (`recorder.py`)
```
asciinema rec --command "python -m demo.player screenplay.yaml --emit-timing /tmp/timing.json" \
  --cols 120 --rows 40 --overwrite /tmp/recording.cast
```
The player runs inside asciinema, which captures all ANSI output with precise timestamps. The `--emit-timing` flag makes the player also write the timing manifest.

**Step 2: Render to raw video** (`recorder.py`)
Two options (try agg first, fall back to ffmpeg):
```
# Option A: agg → GIF → ffmpeg decode (simpler, current approach)
agg --font-size 22 --font-family "JetBrains Mono" --theme dracula \
    --cols 120 --rows 40 --fps-cap 30 /tmp/recording.cast /tmp/raw.gif

# Then decode GIF to raw video:
ffmpeg -i /tmp/raw.gif -c:v libx264 -pix_fmt yuv420p /tmp/raw.mp4
```

**Step 3: Apply camera** (`camera.py`)
Generate an ffmpeg `filter_complex` string from camera keyframes. The key insight: render at **2x resolution**, then crop+scale for camera movements.

For a 2560x1440 render targeting 1280x720 output:
```python
def build_ffmpeg_filter(keyframes, input_w, input_h, out_w, out_h):
    """Build ffmpeg filter_complex for camera movements.
    
    Each keyframe: (time, zoom, cx_norm, cy_norm, ease)
    """
    # Convert normalized coordinates to pixel crop params
    # At zoom=1.0: crop is full frame, scaled to output
    # At zoom=2.0: crop is half frame centered on (cx, cy)
    
    # Use ffmpeg's sendcmd or zoompan filter
    # zoompan is ideal: it handles zoom+pan+interpolation natively
    
    # Build zoompan expression with keyframe interpolation
    zoom_expr = build_interpolated_expr(keyframes, 'zoom')
    x_expr = build_interpolated_expr(keyframes, 'x')
    y_expr = build_interpolated_expr(keyframes, 'y')
    
    return (
        f"zoompan=z='{zoom_expr}':x='{x_expr}':y='{y_expr}'"
        f":d=1:s={out_w}x{out_h}:fps={fps}"
    )
```

The `zoompan` filter is the right ffmpeg tool here. It takes per-frame expressions for zoom, x, y and handles interpolation. We generate the expression strings from our keyframe list.

For easing, ffmpeg's expression language supports `if(between(t,a,b), lerp_expr, hold_expr)` which we can chain. Each transition between keyframes becomes one `if(between(...))` clause.

**Step 4: Encode final** (`pipeline.py`)
```
ffmpeg -i /tmp/raw.mp4 -vf "{zoompan_filter},scale={out_w}:{out_h}" \
  -c:v libx264 -preset slow -crf 18 -pix_fmt yuv420p output.mp4
```

### 5. Skin Engine Integration

The refactored `player.py` replaces all hardcoded ANSI codes with skin lookups:

```python
from hermes_cli.skin_engine import load_skin, SkinConfig

class SkinRenderer:
    """Converts skin colors to ANSI escape sequences for terminal output."""
    
    def __init__(self, skin: SkinConfig):
        self.skin = skin
    
    def ansi(self, color_key: str) -> str:
        """Convert a skin hex color to an ANSI escape sequence."""
        hex_color = self.skin.get_color(color_key)
        if not hex_color:
            return ""
        r, g, b = int(hex_color[1:3], 16), int(hex_color[3:5], 16), int(hex_color[5:7], 16)
        return f"\033[38;2;{r};{g};{b}m"
```

The banner action uses the skin's `banner_logo` and `banner_hero` if available. The spinner uses `skin.get_spinner_list("thinking_faces")` and `skin.get_spinner_list("thinking_verbs")`. Response panels use `skin.get_color("response_border")`.

### 6. Scene Extensibility

Each scene type is a callable registered in a simple dict:

```python
# demo/scenes/__init__.py
from typing import Protocol, Dict, Any

class SceneHandler(Protocol):
    def play(self, item: dict, ctx: "PlaybackContext") -> None: ...
    def estimate_duration(self, item: dict, ctx: "PlaybackContext") -> float: ...

_registry: Dict[str, SceneHandler] = {}

def register(name: str, handler: SceneHandler):
    _registry[name] = handler

def get_handler(item: dict) -> SceneHandler:
    if "action" in item:
        action_name = item["action"]
        if action_name in _registry:
            return _registry[action_name]
        return _registry["_default_action"]
    if "user" in item:
        return _registry["conversation"]
    raise ValueError(f"Unknown scene type: {item}")
```

The `PlaybackContext` dataclass carries shared state: skin renderer, typing speed, timing collector, console. Registration happens in `conversation.py` and `action.py` at import time.

To add a new scene type (e.g., a "split_screen" or "code_highlight"):
1. Create `demo/scenes/my_scene.py`
2. Implement `play()` and `estimate_duration()`
3. Call `register("my_scene", MySceneHandler())`
4. Import it in `demo/scenes/__init__.py`

### 7. CLI Interface

```
# Full pipeline: screenplay → video
python -m demo screenplay.yaml -o output.mp4

# Just play in terminal (development/preview)
python -m demo screenplay.yaml --play

# Record only (no camera post-processing)
python -m demo screenplay.yaml --record-only -o recording.cast

# Apply camera to existing recording
python -m demo --apply-camera recording.cast timing.json screenplay.yaml -o output.mp4

# Options
python -m demo screenplay.yaml -o output.mp4 \
  --skin ares \
  --typing-speed 0.03 \
  --cols 120 --rows 40 \
  --render-width 2560 --render-height 1440 \
  --output-width 1280 --output-height 720
```

The `__main__.py` uses argparse with subcommand-like flags. The default mode (screenplay + output) runs the full pipeline.

### 8. Implementation Order

**Phase 1: Refactor player.py into the package** (foundation)
1. Create `demo/__init__.py`, `demo/__main__.py`
2. Create `demo/schema.py` with Pydantic models for the YAML schema
3. Create `demo/scenes/__init__.py` with the registry and `PlaybackContext`
4. Split `demo/player.py` into `demo/scenes/conversation.py` and `demo/scenes/action.py`
5. Create new `demo/player.py` that loads screenplay, resolves skin, iterates scenes
6. Wire up skin engine integration (replace hardcoded ANSI codes)
7. Verify: `python -m demo scripts/example.yaml --play` produces identical output to old player

**Phase 2: Timing system**
8. Add timing collector to `PlaybackContext` -- wraps `time.sleep()` to record timestamps
9. Add `--emit-timing` flag that writes the JSON manifest after playback
10. Verify: timing JSON has correct structure and timestamps match .cast file

**Phase 3: Recording pipeline**
11. Create `demo/recorder.py` -- subprocess wrapper for asciinema + agg
12. Create `demo/pipeline.py` -- orchestrates record -> render
13. Verify: `python -m demo scripts/example.yaml -o output.mp4` produces a video (no camera yet)

**Phase 4: Camera system**
14. Add camera fields to `demo/schema.py` Pydantic models
15. Create `demo/camera.py` -- resolves camera directives against timing manifest, generates ffmpeg zoompan filter
16. Integrate camera into `pipeline.py` as the post-processing step
17. Verify: a screenplay with `camera:` directives produces zoom/pan effects in output

**Phase 5: Polish**
18. Add `--dry-run` that prints the ffmpeg command without executing
19. Add `--preview` that plays at 2x speed for quick iteration
20. Error handling, progress bars, temp file cleanup

### 9. Key Technical Decisions

**Why zoompan instead of crop+scale**: ffmpeg's `zoompan` filter natively handles smooth interpolation between keyframes. Building this manually with `crop` requires generating per-frame expressions, which zoompan already does.

**Why 2x render resolution**: Camera zoom requires "extra pixels" to zoom into. Rendering at 2560x1440 and outputting 1280x720 means we always have 2x zoom headroom. At zoom=1.0, the full 2560x1440 is downscaled (extra sharp). At zoom=2.0, we're showing native pixels.

**Why asciinema rather than screen capture**: asciinema captures ANSI output losslessly at the terminal level. This means the recording is resolution-independent -- we can render to any size with agg after the fact. Screen capture would lock us to one resolution and introduce compression artifacts.

**Why timing manifest as a sidecar**: The .cast file has timestamps, but they are per-character ANSI events, not semantic events. We need to know "response started at t=9.67" to resolve `camera.at: "response_start"`. The player knows this; the .cast file does not.

**Skin integration depth**: The player uses the skin engine's `load_skin()` directly rather than the global `get_active_skin()`. This avoids dependency on CLI initialization and lets the screenplay specify `skin: "ares"` independently.
